u(h,s)^{\epsilon}a(h,s)\leq u(k,s)^{\epsilon}a(h,s)\leq\frac{Cr^{2\epsilon}m(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa}}{(t-s)^{2\epsilon}(h-k)^{2}}\,u(k,t)^{\epsilon+1}a(k,t)^{\epsilon\kappa}.

Following
[
Sta
]
, we define a function of two variables
\varphi
by

(8.50)

\varphi(h,s)=u(h,s)^{\epsilon}a(h,s)\ \text{ for $h>0$ and }0<s<2r.

Notice that
\varphi(h,s)\geq 0
. When
s
is fixed,
\varphi(h,s)
is non increasing in
h
,
and when
h
is fixed,
\varphi(h,s)
is non decreasing in
s
. We want to show that

(8.51)

\varphi(h,s)\leq\frac{K}{(h-k)^{\alpha}(t-s)^{\gamma}}\left[\varphi(k,t)\right]^{\beta}

for some choice of positive constants
K
,
\alpha
and
\gamma
, and some
\beta>1
,
because if we do so we shall be able to use Lemma 5.1 in
[
Sta
]
directly.

It is a good idea to choose
\epsilon
so that

(8.52)

\left\{\begin{split}\beta\epsilon=\epsilon+1,\\
\beta=\epsilon\kappa.\end{split}\right.

for some
\beta>1
.
Choose
\beta=\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}+\kappa}>1
and
\epsilon=\frac{\beta}{\kappa}>0
. An easy computation proves that
(\epsilon,\beta)
satisfies (
8.52
).
With this choice,
(
8.49
) becomes

(8.53)

\varphi(h,s)\leq\frac{Cr^{2\epsilon}m(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa}}{(t-s)^{2\epsilon}(h-k)^{2}}\varphi(k,t)^{\beta},

which is exactly (
8.51
) with
K=Cr^{2\epsilon}m(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa}
,
\alpha=2

and
\gamma=2\epsilon
.

So we can apply Lemma 5.1 in
[
Sta
]
, which says that

(8.54)

\varphi(\mathfrak{d},r)=0,

where
\mathfrak{d}
is given by

(8.55)

\mathfrak{d}^{\alpha}=\dfrac{2^{\beta\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\beta-1}}K[\varphi(0,2r)]^{\beta-1}}{r^{\gamma}}.

We replace and get that we can take

(8.56)

\mathfrak{d}^{2}=Cr^{2\epsilon}m(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa}\ \frac{\varphi(0,2r)^{\beta-1}}{r^{\gamma}}=Cm(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa}\varphi(0,2r)^{\beta-1}.

Notice that
\varphi(\mathfrak{d},r)=0
implies that

a(\mathfrak{d},r)=0
, which in turn implies that
u\leq\mathfrak{d}
a.e. on
B=B(x,r)
.
Moreover, by definition of
a
, we have
a(0,2r)\leq m(2B)
. Thus

(8.57)

\begin{split}\sup_{B(x,r)}u&\leq\mathfrak{d}\leq Cm(2B)^{-\epsilon\kappa/2}u(0,2r)^{(\beta-1)\varepsilon/2}a(0,2r)^{(\beta-1)/2}\\
&\leq Cu(0,2r)^{\epsilon(\beta-1)/2}m(2B)^{(\beta-1-\epsilon\kappa)/2}.\end{split}

The first line in (
8.52
) yields
\epsilon(\beta-1)=1
and the second line in (
8.52
) yields

\beta-1-\epsilon\kappa=-1
.
Besides,

u(0,2r)=\int_{2B}u^{2}dm

because
u
is nonnegative. Hence

(8.58)

\sup_{B}u\leq C\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}u^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which is the desired conclusion.

\Box

Lemma 8.13

(Moser estimate at the boundary for general
p
)
.

Let
p>0
. Let
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
. Let
u\in W_{r}(2B)
be a non-negative subsolution in
2B\setminus\Gamma
such that
Tu=0
a.e. on
2B
. Then

(8.59)

\sup_{B}u\leq C_{p}\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}u^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
C_{p}
depends only on
the dimensions
n
and
d
, the constants
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
, and the exponent
p
.

Proof
.  Lemma
8.13
can be
deduced
from Lemma
8.12
by a simple iterative argument.
The proof is fairly similar to the very end of the proof of
[
HL
, Chapter IV, Theorem 1.1]
.
Nevertheless, because the proof in
[
HL
]
doesn’t hold at the boundary (and for the sake of completeness), we give a proof here.

First, let us prove that we can improve (
8.39
) into the following: if
B
is a ball centered
on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(B)
is a non-negative subsolution on
B\cap\Omega
such that

Tu=0
a.e. on
B
, then for any
\theta\in(0,1)

(in practice, close to
1
),

(8.60)

\sup_{\theta B}u\leq C(1-\theta)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left(m(B)^{-1}\int_{B}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
C>0
depends only on
n
,
d
,
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
.

Let
B
be a ball
centered on
\Gamma
, with
radius
r
,
and let
\theta\in(0,1)
.
Choose
x\in\theta B
.
Two cases may happen: either
\delta(x)\geq\frac{1-\theta}{6}r
or

\delta(x)<\frac{1-\theta}{6}r
.
In the first case, if

\delta(x)\geq\frac{1-\theta}{6}r
, we apply Lemma
8.7
to the ball

B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{20}r)

(notice that
B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{10}r)\subset B\cap\Omega
). We get that

(8.61)

\begin{split}u(x)&\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m(B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{10}r))}\int_{B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{10}r)}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C\left(\frac{m(B(x,2r))}{m(B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{10}r)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C(1-\theta)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left(m(B)^{-1}\int_{B}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

by
(
2.8
). In the second case, when

\delta(x)\leq\frac{1-\theta}{6}r
, we take
y\in\Gamma
such that
|x-y|=\delta(x)
. Remark that
y\in\frac{1+\theta}{2}B
and then
B(y,\frac{1-\theta}{2}r)\subset B
. We apply then Lemma
8.12
to the ball
B(y,\frac{1-\theta}{6}r)
in order to get

(8.62)

\begin{split}u(x)\leq\sup_{B(y,\frac{1-\theta}{6}r)}u&\leq C\left(\frac{1}{m(B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{3}r))}\int_{B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{3}r)}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C\left(\frac{m(B(x,2r))}{m(B(x,\frac{1-\theta}{3}r)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{m(B)}\int_{B}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C(1-\theta)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left(m(B)^{-1}\int_{B}u^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

with (
2.8
). The claim (
8.60
) follows.

Let us prove now (
8.59
). Without loss of generality, we can
restrict to
the case
p<2
, since the case
p\geq 2
can be deduced from Lemma
8.12
and
Hölder’s
inequality.

Let
B=B(x,r)
be a ball and let
u\in W_{r}(2B)
be a non-negative
subsolution on
2B\setminus\Gamma

such that
Tu=0
on
2B
.
Set for
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

r_{i}:=r\sum_{j=0}^{i}3^{-j}=\frac{3}{2}r(1-3^{-i-1})<\frac{3}{2}r.

Note that
\frac{r_{i}}{r_{i}-r_{i-1}}=\frac{3^{i+1}-1}{2}\leq 3^{i+1}
. As a consequence, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, (
8.60
) yields

(8.63)

\begin{split}\sup_{B(x,r_{i-1})}u&\leq C3^{\frac{in}{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{m(B(x,r_{i}))}\int_{B(x,r_{i})}|u|^{2}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C3^{\frac{in}{2}}\bigg(\sup_{B(x,r_{i})}u\bigg)^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{m(B(x,r_{i}))}\int_{B(x,r_{i})}|u|^{p}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C3^{\frac{in}{2}}\bigg(\sup_{B(x,r_{i})}u\bigg)^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{m(2B)}\int_{B(x,r_{i})}|u|^{p}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\\
\end{split}

Set
\alpha=1-\frac{p}{2}
. By taking the power
\alpha^{i-1}
of the inequality (
8.63
), where
i
is a positive integer, we obtain

(8.64)

\bigg(\sup_{B(x,r_{i-1})}u\bigg)^{\alpha^{i-1}}\leq C^{\alpha^{i-1}}(3^{\frac{in}{2}})^{\alpha^{i-1}}\bigg(\sup_{B(x,r_{i})}u\bigg)^{\alpha^{i}}\bigg(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{B(x,r_{i})}|u|^{p}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{i}},

where
C
is independent of

i
(and also
p
,
x
,
r
and
u
). An immediate induction gives, for any
i\geq 1
,

(8.65)

\sup_{B(x,r)}u\leq C^{\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\alpha^{j}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{i}3^{\frac{jn}{2}\alpha^{j-1}}\bigg)\bigg(\sup_{B(x,r_{i})}u\bigg)^{\alpha^{i}}\bigg(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{B(x,r_{i})}|u|^{p}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\alpha^{j}},

and if we apply Corollary
8.39
once more, we get that

(8.66)

\sup_{B}u\leq C^{\sum_{j=0}^{i}\alpha^{j}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{i+1}3^{\frac{jn}{2}\alpha^{j-1}}\bigg)\bigg(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}|u|^{p}dm\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\alpha^{j}}\bigg(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{\frac{3}{2}B}|u|^{2}dm\bigg)^{\frac{\alpha^{i}}{2}}.

We want to to take the limit when
i
goes to
+\infty
. Since
u\in W_{r}(2B)
, the quantity
\int_{\frac{3}{2}B}|u|^{2}dm
is finite and thus

(8.67)

\lim_{i\to+\infty}\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{\frac{3}{2}B}|u|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{\alpha^{i}}{2}}=1

because we took
p
such that
\alpha=1-\frac{p}{2}<1
. Note also that

(8.68)

\lim_{i\to+\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\alpha^{j}=\frac{2}{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{i\to+\infty}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\alpha^{j}=\frac{1}{p}.

Furthermore,
\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{i+1}3^{\frac{jn}{2}\alpha^{j-1}}
has a limit (that depends on
p
and
n
) when
j<+\infty
because,

(8.69)

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{jn}{2}\alpha^{j-1}=\frac{n}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}j\alpha^{j-1}<+\infty.

These three facts prove that the limit when
i\to+\infty
of the right-hand side of (
8.66
) exists and

(8.70)

\sup_{B}u\leq C_{p}\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}|u|^{p}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

which is the desired result.

\Box

Next comes the Hölder continuity of the solutions at the boundary. We start with a boundary version of the density property.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(4B)
be a non-negative supersolution in

4B\setminus\Gamma
such that
Tu=1
a.e. on
4B
.
Then

(8.71)

\inf_{B}u\geq C^{-1},

where
C>0
depends only on the dimensions
d
,
n
and the constants
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
.

Proof.

The ideas of the proof are taken from the Density Theorem
(Section 4.3, Theorem 4.9) in
[
HL
]
.
The result in
[
HL
]
states, roughly speaking, that (
8.71
) holds whenever
u

is a supersolution in
4B\subset\Omega

such that

u\geq 1
on a large piece of
B
; and its proof relies on a Poincaré inequality on balls
for functions that equal
0

on a big piece of the considered ball.

We will adapt this argument to the case where
B
is centered on
\Gamma
and we will rely on the Poincaré inequality given by Lemma
4.1
.

Let
B
and
u
be as in the statement.
Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be small (it will be used to avoid some functions to take the value
0
)
and set
u_{\delta}=\min\{1,u+\delta\}
and
v_{\delta}:=-\Phi_{\delta}(u_{\delta})
, where
\Phi
is a smooth Lipschitz function defined on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi_{\delta}(s)=-\ln(s)
when
s\in[\delta,1]
.

The plan of the proof is: first we prove that
v_{\delta}
is a subsolution, and then
we use the Moser estimate and the Poincaré inequality given Lemma
8.12
and
4.1
respectively. It will give that the supremum of
v_{\delta}
on
B
is bounded by the
L^{2}
-norm of the gradient of
v_{\delta}
.
Then, we will test the supersolution
u_{\delta}
against an appropriate test function, which will give that the
L^{2}(2B)
bound on
\nabla v_{\delta}
- and thus the supremum of
v_{\delta}
on
B
- can be bounded by a constant independent of
\delta
.
This will yield
a lower bound on
u_{\delta}(x)
which is uniform in
\delta
and
x\in B
.

So we start by proving that

(8.72)

v_{\delta}\in W_{r}(4B)
is a subsolution in
4B\setminus\Gamma

such that

Tv_{\delta}=0
a.e. on
4B
.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega\cap 4B)
.
Choose

\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega\cap 4B)

such that
\phi\equiv 1
on
\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi
. Then for

y\in\Omega
,

(8.73)

v_{\delta}(y)\varphi(y)=\Phi_{\delta}(\min\{1,(u(y)+\delta)\phi(y)\})\varphi(y).

Since
u\in W_{r}(4B)
, it follows that
u\phi\in W
and thus
(u+\delta)\phi\in W
.
Consequently, we obtain
\min\{1,(u+\delta)\phi\}\in W
by
Lemma
6.1
(b), then
\Phi_{\delta}(\min\{1,(u+\delta)\phi\})\in W
by
Lemma
6.1
(a) and finally
v_{\delta}\varphi\in W
thanks to Lemma
5.4
.
Hence

v_{\delta}\in W_{r}(4B)
. Using the fact that the trace is local and Lemmata
6.1
and
8.1
, it is clear that

(8.74)

Tv_{\delta}=-\ln(\min\{1,T(u\phi)+\delta\})=0\ \text{ a.e. on }4B.

The claim (
8.72
) will be proven if we can show that
v_{\delta}
is a subsolution in
4B\setminus\Gamma
. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(4B\setminus\Gamma)
be a non-negative function. We have

(8.75)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla v_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm&=-\int_{\Omega}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}}{u_{\delta}}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm\\
&=-\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\varphi}{u_{\delta}}\right)\,dm-\int_{4B}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla u_{\delta}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}\varphi\,dm.\end{split}

The second term in the right-hand side is non-positive by
the ellipticity condition (
8.9
). So
v_{\delta}
is a subsolution if we can establish that

(8.76)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\varphi}{u_{\delta}}\right)\,dm\geq 0.

Yet
u_{\delta}
is a supersolution according to Lemma
8.5
. Moreover
\varphi/u_{\delta}
is compactly supported in
4B\setminus\Gamma
and, since
u_{\delta}\geq\delta>0
,
we deduce from Lemma
6.1
that
\frac{\varphi}{u_{\delta}}\in W
.
So (
8.76
) is just
a consequence of Lemma
8.3
. The claim (
8.72
) follows.

The function
v_{\delta}
satisfies now all the assumptions of Lemma
8.12
and thus

(8.77)

\sup_{B}v_{\delta}\leq C\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}|v_{\delta}|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
Tv_{\delta}=0
a.e. on
2B
, the right-hand side can be bounded with
the help of (
4.13
),
which gives

(8.78)

\sup_{B}v_{\delta}\leq Cr\left(m(2B)^{-1}\int_{2B}|\nabla v_{\delta}|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We will prove that the right-hand side of (
8.78
) is bounded uniformly in
\delta
.
Use the test function

\varphi=\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{1}{u_{\delta}}-1\big)
with

\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(4B)
,
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
,
\alpha\equiv 1
on
2B
and
\nabla\alpha\leq\frac{1}{r}
. Note that
\varphi
is a non-negative function compactly supported in
4B
and, by
Lemma
6.1
,
\varphi
is in
W
and has zero trace, that is
\varphi\in W_{0}
.

Since
u
is a supersolution,
u_{\delta}
is also a supersolution. We test
u_{\delta}
against
\varphi
(this is allowed,
thanks to Lemma
8.3
) and we get

(8.79)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\Big[\alpha^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{u_{\delta}}-1\Big)\Big]dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}\,\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla u_{\delta}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}dm+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha\left(1-u_{\delta}\right)\,\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\alpha}{u_{\delta}}\,dm,

hence, by the
ellipticity and the boundedness of
A
(see (
8.8
) and (
8.9
)),

(8.80)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}\frac{|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{2}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}dm&\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla u_{\delta}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}dm\\
&\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha\left(1-u_{\delta}\right)\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\alpha}{u_{\delta}}\,dm\\
&\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha\left(1-u_{\delta}\right)\frac{|\nabla u_{\delta}||\nabla\alpha|}{u_{\delta}}\,dm\\
&\leq C\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}\frac{|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{2}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(1-u_{\delta}\right)^{2}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\end{split}

by Cauchy-Schwarz’ inequality. Therefore,

(8.81)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}|\nabla\ln u_{\delta}|^{2}dm=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\alpha^{2}\frac{|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{2}}{u_{\delta}^{2}}dm\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1-u_{\delta})^{2}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}dm

because
0\leq u_{\delta}\leq 1
, and then with our particular choice of
\alpha
,

(8.82)

m^{-1}(2B)\int_{2B}|\nabla v_{\delta}|^{2}dm=m^{-1}(2B)\int_{2B}|\nabla\ln u_{\delta}|^{2}dm\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}.

We inject
this last estimate in (
8.78
)
and get that

(8.83)

\sup_{B}v_{\delta}=\sup_{B}(-\ln u_{\delta})\leq C,

i.e.
\displaystyle\inf_{B}u_{\delta}=\inf_{B}\min\{1,u+\delta\}\geq e^{-C}=C^{-1}
. Since the constant doesn’t depend on
\delta
, we have the right conclusion, that is

\displaystyle\inf_{B}u\geq C^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 8.15

(Oscillation estimates on the boundary)
.

Let
B
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(4B)
be a solution in
4B\setminus\Gamma
such that
Tu
is uniformly bounded on
4B
. Then, there exists
\eta\in(0,1)
such that

(8.84)

\osc_{B}u\leq\eta\osc_{4B}u+(1-\eta)\osc_{\Gamma\cap 4B}Tu.

The constant
\eta
depends only on the dimensions
n
,
d
and the constants
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
.

Proof.

Set
\displaystyle M_{4}=\sup_{4B}u
,
\displaystyle m_{4}=\inf_{4B}u
,
\displaystyle M_{1}=\sup_{B}u
,
\displaystyle m_{1}=\inf_{B}u
,
\displaystyle M=\sup_{4B\cap\Gamma}Tu
and
\displaystyle m=\inf_{4B\cap\Gamma}Tu
.
Let us first prove that

(8.85)

{M_{4}-M_{1}}\geq c({M_{4}-M})

and

(8.86)

{m_{1}-m_{4}}\geq c({m-m_{4}})

for some
c\in(0,1]
. Notice
that (
8.85
) is trivially true if
M_{4}-M=0
. Otherwise, we apply Lemma
8.14
to the non-negative supersolution
\min\{\frac{M_{4}-u}{M_{4}-M},1\}
whose trace equals 1 on
4B
(with Lemma
6.1
) and we obtain for some constant
c\in(0,1]

(8.87)

\frac{M_{4}-M_{1}}{M_{4}-M}\geq c

which gives (
8.85
) if we multiply both sides by
M_{4}-M
. In the same way, (
8.86
) is true if
m-m_{4}=0
and otherwise, we apply Lemma
8.14
to the non-negative supersolution
\min\{\frac{u-m_{4}}{m-m_{4}},1\}
and we get for some
c\in(0,1]

(8.88)

\frac{m_{1}-m_{4}}{m-m_{4}}\geq c,

which is
(
8.86
).

We sum then (
8.85
) and (
8.86
) to get

(8.89)

[M_{4}-m_{4}]-[M_{1}-m_{1}]\geq c[M_{4}-m_{4}]-c[M-m],

that is

(8.90)

[M_{1}-m_{1}]\leq(1-c)[M_{4}-m_{4}]+c[M-m],

which is exactly the desired result.
∎

We end the section with the Hölder continuity of solutions at the boundary.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
B=B(x,r)
be a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
u\in W_{r}(B)
be a solution in
B

such that
Tu
is continuous and bounded on
B
.
There exists
\alpha>0
such that for

0<s<r
,

(8.91)

\osc_{B(x,s)}u\leq C\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\osc_{B(x,r)}u+C\osc_{B(x,\sqrt{sr})\cap\Gamma}Tu

where the constants
\alpha,C
depend only on the dimensions
n
and
d
and the constants
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
. In particular,
u
is continuous on
B
.

If, in addition,
Tu\equiv 0
on
B
, then for any
0<s<r/2

(8.92)

\osc_{B(x,s)}u\leq C\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\left(m(B)^{-1}\int_{B}|u|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

The first part of the Lemma, i.e. the estimate (
8.91
), is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
8.15
and
[
GT
, Lemma 8.23]
. Basically,
[
GT
, Lemma 8.23]
is a result on functions stating that the functional inequality (
8.84
) can be turned, via iterations, into (
8.91
).

The second part of the Lemma is simply a consequence of the first part and of the Moser inequality given in Lemma
8.12
.
∎

Chapter 9
Harmonic Measure

We want to solve the Dirichlet problem

(9.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Lu=f&\text{ in }\Omega\\
u=g&\text{ on }\Gamma,\end{array}\right.

with a notation that we explain now.
Here we require
u
to lie in
W
, and by the second line we actually mean that

Tu=g

\sigma
-almost everywhere on
\Gamma
, where
T
is our trace operator.
Logically, we are only interested in functions
g\in H
, because we know that

T(u)\in H
for
u\in W
.

The condition
Lu=f
in
\Omega
is taken in the weak sense, i.e. we say that
u\in W
satisfies
Lu=f
,
where
f\in W^{-1}=(W_{0})^{*}
, if for any
v\in W_{0}
,

(9.2)

a(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla v=\left<f,v\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}.

Notice that when

f\equiv 0
, a function
u\in W
that satisfies
(
9.2
) is a solution in
\Omega
.

Now, we made sense of (
9.1
) for at least
f\in W^{-1}
and
g\in H
.
The next result gives a good solution to the Dirichlet problem.

Lemma 9.1
.

For any
f\in W^{-1}
and any
g\in H
, there exists a unique
u\in W
such that

(9.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Lu=f&\text{ in }\Omega\\
Tu=g&\text{ a.e. on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
independent of
f
and
g
such that

(9.4)

\|u\|_{W}\leq C(\|g\|_{H}+\|f\|_{W^{-1}}),

where

(9.5)

\|f\|_{W^{-1}}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in W_{0}\\
\|\varphi\|_{W}=1\end{subarray}}\left<f,\varphi\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}.

Proof
.  Since
g\in H
, Theorem
7.3
implies that
there exists
G\in W
such that
T(G)=g
and

(9.6)

\|G\|_{W}\leq C\|g\|_{H}.

The quantity
LG
is an element of
W^{-1}
defined by

(9.7)

\left<LG,\varphi\right>_{W^{-1},W_{0}}:=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla G\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dz=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla G\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm,

and notice that

(9.8)

\|LG\|_{W^{-1}}\leq C\|G\|_{W}\leq C\|g\|_{H}

by
(
8.8
) and (
9.6
).

Observe that
the conditions (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) imply that the bilinear form
a
is
bounded and coercive on
W_{0}
. It follows
from the Lax-Milgram theorem that there exists a (unique)
v\in W_{0}
such that
Lv=-LG-f
.
Set
u=G-v
. It is now easy to see that
Tu=g
a.e. on
\Gamma
and
Lu=f
in
\Gamma
.
The existence of a solution of (
9.3
) follows.

It remains to check the uniqueness of the solution and the bounds (
9.4
).
Take
u_{1},u_{2}\in W
two solutions of (
9.3
). One has then
T(u_{1}-u_{2})=g-g=0
and hence

u_{1}-u_{2}\in W_{0}
. Moreover,
L(u_{1}-u_{2})=0
. Since
a
is bounded and coercive,
the uniqueness in the Lax-Milgram theorem yields
u_{1}-u_{2}=0
.
Therefore (
9.3
) has also a unique solution.

Finally, let us prove the bounds (
9.4
). From the coercivity of
a
, we get that

(9.9)

\|v\|_{W}^{2}\leq Ca(v,v)\leq C\|LG+f\|_{W^{-1}}\|v\|_{W},

i.e., with (
9.8
),

(9.10)

\|v\|_{W}\leq C\|LG+f\|_{W^{-1}}\leq C(\|g\|_{H}+\|f\|_{W^{-1}}).

We conclude the proof of (
9.4
) with

(9.11)

\|u\|_{W}=\|G-v\|_{W}\leq C(\|g\|_{H}+\|f\|_{W^{-1}})

by (
9.6
).

\Box

The next step in the construction of a harmonic measure associated to
L
, is to prove a maximum principle.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
u\in W
be a supersolution in
\Omega
satisfying
Tu\geq 0
a.e. on
\Gamma
. Then
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
.

Proof
.  Set
v=\min\{u,0\}\leq 0
. According to Lemma
6.1
(b), we have

(9.12)

\nabla v=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla u&\text{ if }u<0\\
0&\text{ if }u\geq 0\end{array}\right.

and

(9.13)

Tv=\min\{Tu,0\}=0\quad\text{a.e. in }\Gamma.

In particular, (
9.13
) implies that
v\in W_{0}
.
The third case of Lemma
8.3
allows us to test
v

against the supersolution
u\in W
; this gives

(9.14)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm\leq 0,

that is with (
9.12
),

(9.15)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v\,dm=\int_{\{u<0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dm=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm\leq 0.

Together with the ellipticity condition (
8.9
), we obtain
\|v\|_{W}\leq 0
. Recall
from Lemma
5.2

that
\|.\|_{W}
is a norm on
W_{0}\ni v
, hence
v=0
a.e. in
\Omega
.
We conclude from the definition of
v
that
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
.

\Box

Here is a corollary of Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 9.3

(Maximum principle)
.

Let
u\in W
be a solution
of
Lu=0

in
\Omega
. Then

(9.16)

\sup_{\Omega}u\leq\sup_{\Gamma}Tu

and

(9.17)

\inf_{\Omega}u\geq\inf_{\Gamma}Tu,

where we recall that
\sup
and
\inf
actually essential supremum and infimum.
In particular,
if
Tu
is essentially bounded,

(9.18)

\sup_{\Omega}|u|\leq\sup_{\Gamma}|Tu|.

Proof
.  Let us prove (
9.16
).
Write
M
for the essential supremum of
Tu
on
\Gamma
;
we may assume that
M<+\infty
, because otherwise (
9.16
) is trivial.
Then
M-u\in W
and

T(M-u)\geq 0
a.e. on
\Gamma
.
Lemma
9.2
yields
M-u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
, that is

(9.19)

\sup_{\Omega}u\leq\sup_{\Gamma}Tu.

The lower bound (
9.17
) is similar,
and (
9.18
) follows.

\Box

We want to define the harmonic measure via the Riesz representation theorem (for measures),
that requires a linear form on
the space of compactly supported continuous functions on
\Gamma
. We
denote this space by
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
; that is,
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
if
g
is defined
and continuous on
\Gamma
,
and there exists a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered on
\Gamma
such that

\mathrm{supp}\,g\subset B\cap\Gamma
.

Lemma 9.4
.

There exists a bounded linear operator

(9.20)

U:C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\to C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that, for every every
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
,

(i)

the restriction of
Ug
to
\Gamma
is
g
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Ug=\sup_{\Gamma}g
and
\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Ug=\inf_{\Gamma}g
;

(iii)

Ug\in W_{r}(\Omega)
and is a solution of
L
in
\Omega
;

(iv)

if
B
is a ball centered on
\Gamma
and
g\equiv 0
on
B
, then

Ug
lies in
W_{r}(B)
;

(v)

if
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
, then
Ug\in W
, and it is the solution of (
9.3
),
with
f=0
, provided by Lemma
9.1
.

Proof
.  This is essentially an argument of extension from a dense class by uniform continuity.
We first define
U
on
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
, by saying that
u=Ug
is
the solution of (
9.3
), with
f=0
, provided by Lemma
9.1
. Thus
u\in W
;
but since its trace is
Tu=g
is continuous, it follows from
Lemmata
8.8
and
8.16
(the Hölder continuity inside and at the boundary)
that
u
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Next we check that
U
is linear and bounded on
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H\subset C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)

(where we use the sup norm). The linearity comes from the uniqueness in Lemma
9.1
, and the
boundedness from the maximum principle: for
g,h\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
,
we can apply (
9.19
) to
u=Ug-Uh
, and we get that

\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u|=\sup_{\Omega}|u|\leq\sup_{\Gamma}|Tu|=||g-h||_{\infty}

because
u
is continuous.

It is clear that
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\cap H
is dense in
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
, because
(restrictions to
\Gamma
of) compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(or even Lipschitz functions, for that matter) lie in
H
: compute their norm in (
1.5
) directly.
Thus
U
has a unique extension by continuity to
C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
.
We could even define
U
, with the same properties, on its closure (continuous functions that tend
to
0
at infinity), but we decided not to bother.

We are now ready to check the various properties of
U
. Given
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
, select a sequence

(g_{k})
of compactly supported smooth functions that converges to
g
in the sup norm. Then
u_{k}=Ug_{k}

converges uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
u=Ug
, and in particular
u
is continuous and its restriction to
\Gamma

is
g
, as in (i). In addition, (ii) holds because

\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u_{k}\leq\lim_{k\to+\infty}\sup_{\Gamma}g_{k}=\sup_{\Gamma}g
, and similarly for the infimum.

For (iii) we first need to check that
u\in W_{r}(\Omega)
.
Observe that we know these facts for the
u_{k}
, so we’ll only need to take limits.
Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
be given. Lemma
8.6
(Caccioppoli’s inequality)
says that, since
u_{k}
is a solution,

(9.21)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\phi u_{k})|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}\phi^{2}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}dm+C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u_{k}|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u_{k}|^{2}dm.

The right-hand side of (
9.21
) converges to
C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u|^{2}dm
,
since
|\nabla\phi|^{2}
is bounded and compactly supported.
So
\int_{B}|\nabla(\phi u_{k})|^{2}dm
is bounded uniformly in
k
.
Since the
\phi u_{k}
vanish outside of the support of
\phi
(which lies far from
\Gamma
)
and converge uniformly to
\phi u
, we get that the
\phi u_{k}
converge to
\phi u
in
L^{1}
and,
since the
|\nabla(\phi u_{k})|
are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega,w)
, we can find a subsequence
for which they converge weakly to a limit
V\in L^{2}(\Omega,w)
.
We easily check on test functions that
\nabla(\phi u)=V
, hence
\phi u\in W
for any

\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
, and
u\in W_{r}(\Omega)
.

Next we check that
u
is a solution in
\Omega
, i.e., that for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,

(9.22)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=0.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
be given, and choose
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
such that

\phi\equiv 1
on
\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi
. We just proved that for some subsequence,

\nabla(\phi u_{k})
converges weakly to
\nabla(\phi u)
in
L^{2}(\Omega,w)
. Since
u_{k}
is a solution
for every
k
,

(9.23)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla(\phi u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla(\phi u_{k})\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{k}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=0.

This proves (
9.22
) and (iii) follows.

For (iv), suppose in addition that
g\equiv 0
on a ball
B
centered on
\Gamma
;
we want similar results in
B
(that is, across
\Gamma
).
Notice that it is easy to approximate it (in the supremum norm) by smooth,
compactly supported functions
g_{k}
that also vanish on
\Gamma\cap B
.
Let use such a sequence
(g_{k})
to define
Ug=\lim_{k\to+\infty}Ug_{k}
.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
be given, and let us check that
\varphi u\in W
.
Set
K=\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi
, suppose
K\neq\emptyset
, and set
\delta=\,\mathrm{dist}(K,\partial B)>0
.
Cover
K\cap\Gamma
by a finite number of balls balls
B_{i}
of radius
10^{-1}\delta
centered on

K\cap\Gamma
, and then cover
K^{\prime}=K\setminus\cup_{i}B_{i}
by a finite number of balls
B_{j}
of radius

10^{-2}\delta
centered on that set
K^{\prime}
. We can use a partition of unity composed of smooth functions
supported in the
2B_{i}
and the
2B_{j}
to reduce to the case when
\varphi
is supported
on a
2B_{i}
or a
2B_{j}
.

Suppose for instance that
\varphi
is supported in
2B_{i}
. We can apply Lemma
8.11

(Caccioppoli’s inequality at the boundary) to
u_{k}=Ug_{k}
on the ball
2B_{i}
,
because its trace
g_{k}
vanishes on
4B_{i}
. We get that

(9.24)

\int_{2B_{i}}|\nabla(\varphi u_{k})|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{2B_{i}}(|\varphi\nabla u_{k})|^{2}+|u_{k}\nabla\varphi|^{2})dm\leq\int_{4B_{i}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{k}|^{2}dm.

With this estimate, we can proceed as with (
9.21
) above to prove that
\varphi u\in W

and its derivative is the weak limit of the
\nabla(\varphi u_{k})
. When instead
\varphi
is supported in
a
2B_{j}
, we use the interior Caccioppoli inequality (Lemma
8.6
and proceed as above).

Thus
u=Ug
lies in
W_{r}(B)
, and this proves (iv). We started the proof with (v), so this completes our proof of Lemma
9.4
.

\Box

Our next step is the construction of
the harmonic measure. Let
X\in\Omega
. By Lemma
9.4
, the linear form

(9.25)

g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)\to Ug(X)

is bounded and positive
(because
u=Ug
is nonnegative when
g\geq 0
).
The following statement is thus a direct consequence of the Riesz representation theorem (see for instance
[
Rud
, Theorem 2.14]
).

Lemma 9.5
.

There exists a unique positive regular Borel measure
\omega^{X}
on
\Gamma
such that

(9.26)

Ug(X)=\int_{\Gamma}g(y)d\omega^{X}(y)

for any
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
. Besides, for any Borel set
E\subset\Gamma
,

(9.27)

\omega^{X}(E)=\sup\{\omega^{X}(K):\,E\supset K,\,K\text{ compact}\}=\inf\{\omega^{X}(V):\,E\subset V,\,V\text{ open}\}.

The harmonic measure is a probability measure, as proven in the following result.

Lemma 9.6
.

For any
X\in\Omega
,

\omega^{X}(\Gamma)=1.

Proof
.  Let
X\in\Omega
be given.
Choose
x\in\Gamma
such that
\delta(X)=|X-x|
. Set then
B_{j}=B(x,2^{j}\delta(X))
.
According to (
9.27
),

(9.28)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\omega^{X}(\overline{B_{j}}).

Choose, for

j\geq 1
,
\bar{g}_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{j+1})
such that
0\leq\bar{g}_{j}\leq 1
and
\bar{g}_{j}\equiv 1
on
\overline{B_{j}}
and then define
g_{j}=T(\bar{g}_{j})
. Since the harmonic measure is positive, we have

(9.29)

\omega^{X}(B_{j})\leq\int_{\Gamma}g_{j}(y)d\omega^{X}(y)\leq\omega^{X}(B_{j+1}).

Together with (
9.28
),

(9.30)

\omega^{X}(\Gamma)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\Gamma}g_{j}(y)d\omega^{X}(y)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}u_{j}(X),

where
u_{j}
is the image by the map (
9.20
) of the function
g_{j}
.
Since
g_{j}
is the trace of a smooth and compactly supported function,
g_{j}\in H
and so
u_{j}\in W
is the solution of (
9.3
) with data
g_{j}
. Moreover,

0\leq u_{j}\leq 1
by
Lemma
9.4
(ii). We want to show that
u_{j}(X)\to 1
when
j\to+\infty
.
The function
v_{j}:=1-u_{j}\in W
is a solution in
B_{j}
satisfying
Tv_{j}\equiv 0
on
B_{j}
.
So Lemma
8.16
says that

(9.31)

0\leq 1-u_{j}(X)=v_{j}(X)\leq\osc_{B_{1}}v_{j}\leq C2^{-j\alpha}\osc_{B_{j}}v_{j}\leq C2^{-j\alpha},

where
C>0
and
\alpha>0
are independent of
j
. It follows that
v_{j}(X)
tends to
0
,
and

u_{j}(X)
tends to
1

when
j
goes to
+\infty
.
The lemma follows from this and (
9.30
), the lemma follows.

\Box

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
E\subset\Gamma
be a Borel set and define the function
u_{E}
on
\Omega
by
u_{E}(X)=\omega^{X}(E)
.
Then

(i)

if there exists
X\in\Omega
such that
u_{E}(X)=0
, then
u_{E}\equiv 0
;

(ii)

the function
u_{E}
lies
in
W_{r}(\Omega)
and is a solution in
\Omega
;

(iii)

if
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a ball such that

E\cap B=\emptyset
, then
u_{E}\in W_{r}(B)
and
Tu_{E}=0
on
B
.

Proof
.  First of all,

0\leq u_{E}\leq 1
because
\omega^{X}
is a positive probability measure for any
X\in\Omega
.

Let us prove (i). Thanks to (
9.27
), it suffices to prove the result when
E=K
is compact.

Let
X\in\Omega
be such that
u_{K}(X)=0
.
Let
Y\in\Omega
and
\epsilon>0
be given.
By (
9.27
) again,
we can find an open
U
such that
U\supset K
and

\omega^{X}(U)<\epsilon
.
Urysohn’s lemma (see for instance Lemma 2.12 in
[
Rud
]
) gives the existence of
g\in C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
such that
0\leq g\leq 1
and
g\equiv 1
on
K
.
Set
u=Ug
, where
U
is as in (
9.20
).
Thanks to the positivity of the harmonic measure,
u_{K}\leq u
.
Let
Y\in\Omega
be given, and apply the Harnack inequality (
8.35
) to
u

(notice that
u
lies in
W_{r}(\Omega)
and is
a solution in
\Omega
thanks to Lemma
9.4
). We get that

(9.32)

0\leq u_{K}(Y)\leq u(Y)\leq C_{X,Y}u(X)\leq C_{X,Y}\epsilon.

Since (
9.32
) holds for any positive
\epsilon
, we have
u_{K}(Y)=0
. Part (i) of the lemma follows.

We turn to the proof of (ii),
which we first do when

E=V
is open.
We first check that

(9.33)

u_{V}
is a continuous function on
\Omega
.

Fix
X\in\Omega
, and build an increasing sequence of compact sets
K_{j}\subset V

such that
\omega^{X}(V)<\omega^{X}(K_{j})+\frac{1}{j}
.
With Urysohn’s lemma again, we construct
g_{j}\in C^{0}_{0}(V)
such that

{\mathds{1}}_{K_{j}}\leq g_{j}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{V}
and,
without loss of generality we can choose
g_{j}\leq g_{i}
whenever
j\leq i
.
Set
u_{j}=Ug_{j}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, as in (
9.20
), and notice that

u_{j}(X)=\int_{\Gamma}g_{j}d\omega^{X}
by (
9.26
). Then for
j\geq 1
,

(9.34)

u_{K_{j}}(X)=\omega^{X}(K_{j})\leq u_{j}(X)\leq\omega^{X}(V)=u_{V}(X)\leq\omega^{X}(K_{j})+\frac{1}{j}

by definition of
u_{E}
, because the harmonic measure is nondecreasing,
and since
{\mathds{1}}_{K_{j}}\leq g_{j}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{V}
.
Similarly,
(u_{j})
is a nondecreasing sequence of functions, i.e.,

(9.35)

u_{i}\geq u_{j}\text{ on $\Omega\,$ for }i\geq j\geq 1,

by the maximum principle in Lemma
9.4
and because
g_{i}\geq g_{j}
, so that in particular

(9.36)

u_{j}(X)\leq u_{i}(X)\leq u_{j}(X)+\frac{1}{j}\ \ \text{ for }i\geq j\geq 1,

by (
9.34
).
Now
u_{i}-u_{j}
is a nonnegative solution (by Lemma
9.4
), and Lemma
8.10

implies that for every
compact set
J\subset\Omega
, there exists
C_{J}>0
such that

(9.37)

0\leq\sup_{J}(u_{i}-u_{j})\leq C_{J}(u_{i}-u_{j})(X)\leq\frac{C_{J}}{j}

for
i\geq j\geq 1
.
We deduce from this
that
(u_{j})_{j}
converges uniformly on compact sets of
\Omega
to a function
u_{\infty}
,
which is therefore continuous on
\Omega
. Thus (
9.33
) will follow as soon as we prove that

u_{\infty}=u_{V}
.

Set
K=\bigcup_{j}K_{j}
; then
u_{K_{j}}\leq u_{K}\leq u_{V}
by monotonicity of the harmonic measure,
and (
9.34
) implies that
u_{K}(X)=u_{V}(X)
. Now
u_{V}-u_{K}=u_{V\setminus K}
, so

u_{V\setminus K}(X)=0
. By Point (i) of the present lemma,
u_{V\setminus K}(Y)=0
for every
Y\in\Omega
.
But
u_{V}(Y)=\omega^{Y}(V)
, and
\omega^{Y}
is a measure, so

u_{V\setminus K}(Y)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}u_{V\setminus K_{j}}(Y)=u_{V}(Y)-\lim_{j\to+\infty}u_{K_{j}}(Y)
.

Since
u_{K_{j}}(Y)\leq u_{j}(Y)\leq u_{V}(Y)
by the proof of (
9.34
), we get that
u_{j}(Y)
tends
to
u_{V}(Y)
. In other words,
u_{\infty}(Y)=u_{V}(Y)
, and (
9.33
) follows as announced.

We proved that
u_{V}
is continuous on
\Omega
and that
it is the limit, uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega
, of a
sequence of functions
u_{j}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap W_{r}(\Omega)
, which are also
solutions of
L
in
\Omega
. We now want to prove
that
u_{V}\in W_{r}(\Omega)
, and we proceed as we did near (
9.21
).

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
be given. In the distributional sense, we have
\nabla(\phi u_{j})=u_{j}\nabla\phi+\phi\nabla u_{j}
. So the
Caccioppoli inequality given by Lemma
8.6
yields

(9.38)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\phi u_{j})|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}(|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u_{j}|^{2}+\phi^{2}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2})dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u_{j}|^{2}dm.

Since the
u_{j}
converge
to
u
uniformly on
\mathrm{supp}\,\phi
,
the right-hand side of (
9.38
) converges to
C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}|u|^{2}dm
.
Consequently, the left-hand side of (
9.38
) is uniformly bounded in
j
and hence there exists
v\in L^{2}(\Omega,w)
such that
\nabla(\phi u_{j})
converges weakly to
v
in
L^{2}(\Omega,w)
. By uniqueness of the limit, the distributional derivative
\nabla(\phi u_{V})
equals
v\in L^{2}(\Omega,w)
, so by definition of
W
,
\phi u_{V}\in W
. Since the result holds for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
, we just established
u_{V}\in W_{r}(\Omega)
as desired.
In addition, we also checked that (for a subsequence)
\nabla(\phi u_{j})

converges weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega,w)
to
\nabla(\phi u_{V})
.

We now establish that
u_{V}
is a solution. Let

\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
be given. Choose

\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
such that
\phi\equiv 1
on
\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi
. Thanks to the weak
convergence of
\nabla(\phi u_{j})
to
\nabla(\phi u_{j})

(9.39)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{V}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm&=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla(\phi u_{V})\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm\\
&=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla(\phi u_{j})\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u_{j}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=0\\
\end{split}

because each
u_{j}
is a solution. Hence
u_{V}
is a solution.

This completes our proof of (ii) when
E=V
is open.
The proof of (ii) for general Borel sets
E
works similarly,
but we now approximate
E
from above by open sets. Fix
X\in\Omega
. Thanks to
the regularity property (
9.27
), there exists a decreasing sequence
(V_{j})
of open sets that
contain
E
, and for which
u_{V_{j}}(X)
tends to
u_{E}(X)
.

From our previous work, we know that each

u_{V_{j}}
is continuous on
\Omega
, lies in
W_{r}(\Omega)
, and is a solution in
\Omega
.
Using the same process as before, we can show first that the
u_{V_{j}}
converge, uniformly on
compact sets of
\Omega
, to
u_{E}
, which is then continuous on
\Omega
.
Then we prove that, for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,
\nabla(\phi u_{V_{j}})
converges weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega,w)
to
\nabla(\phi u_{E})
, from which we deduce
u_{E}\in W_{r}(\Omega)
and then that
u_{E}
is a solution.

Part (iii) of the lemma remains to be proven. Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball such that
B\cap E=\emptyset
. Since
u_{E}
lies in
W_{r}(\Omega)

and is a solution,
Lemma
8.8
says that
u_{E}
is continuous in
\Omega
.
We first prove that if we set
u=0
on
B\cap\Gamma
, we get a continuous extension of
u
,
(with then has a vanishing trace, or restriction, on
B\cap\Gamma
).

Let
x\in B\cap\Gamma
be given.
Choose
r>0
such that
B(x,2r)\subset B
and then construct a function
\bar{g}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B(x,2r))

such that
\bar{g}\equiv 1
in
B(x,r)
. Since
\bar{g}
is smooth and compactly supported,

g:=T(\bar{g})
lies in

H\cap C^{0}_{0}(\Gamma)
and then

u=Ug
, the image of
g
by the map of (
9.20
), lies in
in
W\cap C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. From the positivity of the harmonic measure, we deduce that

0\leq u_{E}\leq 1-u
.
Since
0
and
1-u
are both continuous functions that are equal
0
at
x
,
the squeeze theorem says that

u_{E}
is continuous (or can be extended by continuity) at
x
, and
u_{E}(x)=0
.

To complete the proof of the lemma, we show that
u_{E}
actually lies in
W_{r}(B)
.
As for the proof of (ii),
we first assume that
E=V
is open.
We take a nondecreasing sequence of compact sets
K_{j}\subset V
that converges to
V
,
and then we build
g_{j}\in C_{0}^{0}(V)
,
such that
{\mathds{1}}_{K_{j}}\leq g_{j}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{V}
and the sequence
(g_{j})
is non-decreasing.
We then take
u_{j}=Ug_{j}
(with the map from (
9.20
)), and in particular the
sequence
(u_{j})
is non-decreasing on
\Omega
.
From the proof of (ii), we know that
u_{j}
converges to
u_{V}
on compact sets of
\Omega
,
then in particular
u_{j}
converges pointwise to
u_{V}
in
\Omega
.

Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
;
we want to prove that
\varphi u_{V}\in W
. From Lemma
8.11
, we have

(9.40)

\int_{B}|\nabla(\varphi u_{j})|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{B}(|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{j}|^{2}+\varphi^{2}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2})dm\leq C\int_{B}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{j}|^{2}dm.

Since
u
is continuous on
B
,
u_{V}\in L^{2}(\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi,w)
and the right-hand side
converges to
C\int_{B}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{V}|^{2}dm
by
the dominated convergence theorem. The left-hand side is thus uniformly bounded in
j
and

\nabla(\varphi u_{j})
converges weakly,
maybe after extracting a subsequence,
to some
v
in
L^{2}(B,w)
.
By uniqueness of the limit,
v=\nabla(\varphi u_{V})\in L^{2}(B,w)
. Since the result holds for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
, we get
u_{V}\in W_{r}(B)
.

In the general case where
E
is a Borel set, fix
X\in\Omega
and take a
decreasing sequence of open sets
V_{j}\supset X
such that

u_{V_{j}}(X)\to u_{E}(X)
.
We can prove using part (i) of this lemma that
u_{V_{j}}
converges to
u_{E}
pointwise in
\Omega
.
Then we use Lemma
8.11
to show that for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B)
,

(9.41)

\int_{B}|\nabla(\varphi u_{V_{j}})|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{B}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{V_{j}}|^{2}dm

when
j
is so large that
V_{j}
is far from the support of
\varphi
.
The right-hand side has a limit, thanks to the dominated convergence theorem, thus the left-hand side is uniformly bounded in
j
. So there exists a subsequence of
\nabla(\varphi u_{V_{j}})
that converges weakly in
L^{2}(B,w)
, and by uniqueness to the limit, the limit has to be
\nabla(\varphi u_{E})
, which thus lies
in
L^{2}(B,w)
. We deduce that

\varphi u_{E}\in W
and then
u\in W_{r}(B)
.

\Box

Chapter 10
Green Functions

The aim of this section is to define a Green function, that is, formally, a function
g

defined on
\Omega\times\Omega
and such that for

y\in\Omega
,

(10.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Lg(.,y)=\delta_{y}\quad\text{ in }\Omega\\
Tg(.,y)=0\quad\text{ on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

where
\delta_{y}
denotes the Dirac distribution.

Our proof of existence and uniqueness, and the estimates below, are adapted from
arguments of
[
GW
]
(see also
[
HoK
]
and
[
DK
]
) for the classical case of codimension
1
.

Lemma 10.1
.

There exists a non-negative function
g:\ \Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

with the following properties.

(i)

For any
y\in\Omega
and
any function
\alpha\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
\alpha\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of
y

(10.2)

(1-\alpha)g(.,y)\in W_{0}.

In particular,
g(.,y)\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\})
and
T[g(.,y)]=0
.

(ii)

For
every choice of
y\in\Omega
,
R>0
, and
q\in[1,\frac{n}{n-1})
,

(10.3)

g(.,y)\in W^{1,q}(B(y,R)):=\{u\in L^{q}(B(y,R)),\,\nabla u\in L^{q}(B(y,R))\}.

(iii)

For

y\in\Omega
and

\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,

(10.4)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla_{x}g(x,y)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)\,dx=\varphi(y).

In particular,
g(.,y)
is a solution
of
Lu=0

in
\Omega\setminus\{y\}
.

In addition, the following bounds hold.

(iv)

For

r>0
,

y\in\Omega
and

\epsilon>0
,

(10.5)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B(y,r)}|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|^{2}dm(x)\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Cr^{1-d}&\text{ if }4r\geq\delta(y)\\
\frac{Cr^{2-n}}{w(y)}&\text{ if }2r\leq\delta(y),\,n\geq 3\\
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{w(y)}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}&\text{ if }2r\leq\delta(y),\,n=2,\end{array}\right.

where
C>0
depends on
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{\epsilon}>0

depends on
d
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
, and
\epsilon
.

(v)

For

x,y\in\Omega
such that

x\neq y
and

\epsilon>0
,

(10.6)

0\leq g(x,y)\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C|x-y|^{1-d}&\text{ if }4|x-y|\geq\delta(y)\\
\frac{C|x-y|^{2-n}}{w(y)}&\text{ if }2|x-y|\leq\delta(y),\,n\geq 3\\
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{w(y)}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|}\right)^{\epsilon}&\text{ if }2|x-y|\leq\delta(y),\,n=2,\end{array}\right.

where again
C>0
depends on
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{\epsilon}>0
depends on
d
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
,
\epsilon
.

(vi)

For

q\in[1,\frac{n}{n-1})
and

R\geq\delta(y)
,

(10.7)

\int_{B(y,R)}|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|^{q}dm(x)\leq C_{q}R^{d(1-q)+1},

where
C_{q}>0
depends on
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
, and
q
.

(vii)

For

y\in\Omega
,

R\geq\delta(y)
,

t>0
and

p\in[1,\frac{2n}{n-2}]
(if
n\geq 3
) or
p\in[1,+\infty)
(if
n=2
),

(10.8)

\frac{m(\{x\in B(y,R),\,g(x,y)>t\})}{m(B(y,R))}\leq C_{p}\left(\frac{R^{1-d}}{t}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}},

where
C_{p}>0
depends on
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
p
.

(viii)

For

y\in\Omega
,

t>0
and

\eta\in(0,2)
,

(10.9)

m(\{x\in\Omega,\,|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|>t\})\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Ct^{-\frac{d+1}{d}}&\text{ if }t\leq\delta(y)^{-d}\\
Cw(y)^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}t^{-\frac{n}{n-1}}&\text{ if }t\geq\delta(y)^{-d},\,n\geq 3\\
C_{\eta}w(y)^{-1}\delta(y)^{d\eta}t^{\eta-2}&\text{ if }t\geq\delta(y)^{-d},\,n=2,\end{array}\right.

where
C>0
depends on
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{\eta}>0
depends on
d
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
,
\eta
.

Remark 10.2
.

When
d<1
and
|x-y|\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta(y)
, the bound
g(x,y)\leq C|x-y|^{1-d}

given in (
10.6
) can be improved into

(10.10)

g(x,y)\leq C\min\{\delta(x),\delta(y)\}^{1-d}.

This fact is proven in Lemma
11.4
below.

Remark 10.3
.

The authors believe that the bounds given in (
10.5
) and (
10.6
) when
n=2
and
2r

(or
2|x-y|
) is smaller than
\delta(y)
are not optimal. One should be able to replace for instance the bound
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{w(y)}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}
by
\frac{C}{w(y)}\ln\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)
in (
10.5
) by adapting the arguments of
[
DK
]
(see also
[
FJK
, Theorem 3.3]
). However, the estimates given above are sufficient for our purposes and we didn’t want to make this article even longer.

Remark 10.4
.

Note that when

n\geq 3
, thanks to Lemma
2.3
, the bound (
10.6
) can be gathered into a single estimate

(10.11)

g(x,y)\leq C\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,{|x-y|}))}

whenever
x,y\in\Omega
,
x\neq y
. In the same way, also for
n\geq 3
, the bound (
10.5
) can be gathered into a single estimate

(10.12)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B(y,r)}|\nabla_{x}g(x,y)|^{2}dm(x)\leq C\frac{r^{2}}{m(B(y,r))}

whenever
y\in\Omega
and
r>0
.

Proof
.  This proof will adapt the arguments of
[
GW
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Let
y\in\Omega
be fixed. Consider again the bilinear form
a
on
W_{0}\times W_{0}
defined as

(10.13)

a(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla v=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dm.

The bilinear form
a
is bounded and coercive on
W_{0}
, thanks to (
8.8
) and (
8.9
).

Let
\rho>0
be small. Take, for instance,
\rho
such that
100\rho<\delta(y)
. Write
B_{\rho}
for
B(y,\rho)
. The linear form

(10.14)

\varphi\in W_{0}\to\fint_{B_{\rho}}\varphi(z)\,dz

is bounded. Indeed, let
z
be a point in
\Gamma
, then

(10.15)

\left|\fint_{B_{\rho}}\varphi(z)\,dz\right|\leq C_{y,z,\rho}\fint_{B(z,|y-z|+\rho)}|\varphi(z)|\,dz\leq C_{y,z,\rho}\|\varphi\|_{W}

by
Lemma
4.1
. By the Lax-Milgram theorem, there exists then a unique function
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=g^{\rho}(.,y)\in W_{0}
such that

(10.16)

a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\varphi)=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=\fint_{B_{\rho}}\varphi(z)\,dz\qquad\forall\varphi\in W_{0}.

We like
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
, and will actually spend some time studying it, because
g(\cdot,y)
will later
be obtained as a limit of the
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
. By (
10.16
),

(10.17)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
is a solution of
L\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=0
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
.

This fact will be useful later on.

For now, let us prove that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\geq 0
a.e. on
\Omega
. Since
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
, Lemma
6.1
yields
|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\in W_{0}
,
\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|=\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
a.e. on
\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}>0\}
,
\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|=-\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
a.e. on
\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}<0\}
and
\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|=0
a.e. on
\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=0\}
. Consequently

(10.18)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\cdot\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\,dm=\int_{\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}>0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm+\int_{\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}<0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm

and

(10.19)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm=\int_{\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}>0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm-\int_{\{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}<0\}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\,dm,

which can be rewritten
a(|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|,|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|)=a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})
and
a(|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})=a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|)
. Moreover, if we use
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in W_{0}
and
|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\in W_{0}
as test functions in (
10.16
), we obtain

(10.20)

a(|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|,|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|)=a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})=\int_{B_{\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)\,dz\leq\int_{B_{\rho}}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)|\,dz=a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|)=a(|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}).

Hence
a(|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|-\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})\leq 0
and, by
the coercivity of
a
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\geq 0
a.e. on
\Omega
.

Let
R\geq\delta(y)>100\rho>0
. We write again
B_{R}
for
B(y,R)
. Let
p
in the range given by Lemma
4.2
, that is
p\in[1,2n/(n-2)]
if
n\geq 3
and
p\in[1,+\infty)
if
n=2
.
We aim to prove that for all
t>0
,

(10.21)

\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)>t\})}{m(B_{R})}\leq Ct^{-\frac{p}{2}}R^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}

with a constant
C
independent of
\rho
,
t
and
R
.

We use (
10.16
) with the test function

(10.22)

\varphi(x):=\left(\frac{2}{t}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)}\right)^{+}=\max\left\{0,\frac{2}{t}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)}\right\}

(and
\varphi(x)=0
if
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)=0
), which lies in
W_{0}
by Lemma
6.1
.
So if
\Omega_{s}:=\{x\in\Omega,\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)>s\}
, we have

(10.23)

a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho},\varphi)=\int_{\Omega_{t/2}}\frac{\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}}\,dm=\fint_{B_{r}}\varphi\leq\frac{2}{t}.

Therefore, with the ellipticity condition (
8.9
),

(10.24)

\int_{\Omega_{t/2}}\frac{|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}}{(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{t}.

Pick
y_{0}\in\Gamma
such that

|y-y_{0}|=\delta(y)
. Set
\widetilde{B}_{R}
for
B(y_{0},2R)\supset B_{R}
.
Also define
v
by

v(x):=(\ln(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x))-\ln t+\ln 2)^{+}
, which lies in
W_{0}
too,
thanks to Lemma
6.1
.
The Sobolev-Poincaré inequality (
4.13
) implies that

(10.25)

\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2}\cap\widetilde{B}_{R}}|v|^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq CR\,m(\widetilde{B}_{R})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{t/2}\cap\widetilde{B}_{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq CR\,m(\widetilde{B}_{R})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}

by
(
10.24
). Since
m(\widetilde{B}_{R})\approx R^{d+1}
thanks to Lemma
2.3
, one has

(10.26)

\int_{\Omega_{t/2}\cap B_{R}}\left|\ln\left(\frac{2\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}}{t}\right)\right|^{p}\,dm\leq CR^{p+(d+1)(1-\frac{p}{2})}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

But the latter implies, since
v>\ln 2
on
\Omega_{t}
, that

(10.27)

(\ln 2)^{p}m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})\leq CR^{p+(d+1)(1-\frac{p}{2})}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}=Ct^{-\frac{p}{2}}R^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)+(d+1)}.

The claim (
10.21
) follows once we notice that, due to Lemma
2.3
,
we have
m(B_{R})\approx R^{d+1}
.

Now
we give a pointwise estimate on
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
when
x
is far from
y
. We claim that

(10.28)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C|x-y|^{1-d}\qquad\text{ if }4|x-y|\geq\delta(y)>100\rho,

where again
C>0
is independent of
\rho
.
Set
R=4|x-y|>\delta(y)
. Recall (
10.17
), i.e., that

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
lies
in
W_{0}
and is a solution in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
.
So we can use the Moser estimates to get that

(10.29)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\frac{1}{m(B(x,R/2))}\int_{B(x,R/2)}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm.

Indeed, (
10.29
) is obtained with Lemma
8.7
when
\delta(x)\geq R/30
(apply Moser inequality in the ball
B(x,R/90)
) and with Lemma
8.13
when
\delta(x)\leq R/30
(apply Moser inequality in the ball
B(x_{0},R/15)
where
x_{0}
is such that
|x-x_{0}|=\delta(x)
).

We can use now the fact that
B(x,R/2)\subset B_{R}
and
[
Duo
, p. 28, Proposition 2.3]
to get

(10.30)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(B_{R})}dt

Take
s>0
, to be chosen later.
By
(
10.21
), applied with any
valid
p>2
(for instance
p=\frac{2n}{n-1}
),

(10.31)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)&\leq C\int_{0}^{s}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(B_{R})}dt+C\int_{s}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(B_{R})}dt\\
&\leq Cs+CR^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}\int_{s}^{+\infty}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}dt\leq Cs+CR^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}s^{1-\frac{p}{2}}.\end{split}

We minimize the right-hand
side in
s
. We find
s\approx R^{1-d}
and then
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq CR^{1-d}
. The claim (
10.28
) follows.

Let us now prove some
pointwise estimates on
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
when
x
is close to
y
.
When
n\geq 3
, we want to show that

(10.32)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\frac{|x-y|^{2-n}}{w(y)}\qquad\text{ if }\delta(y)\geq 2|x-y|>4\rho\text{ and }\delta(y)>100\rho,

where
C>0
is independent of
\rho
,
x
and
y
.
When
n=2
,
we claim that for any
\epsilon>0
,

(10.33)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C_{\epsilon}\frac{1}{w(y)}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}\qquad\text{ if }\delta(y)\geq 2|x-y|>4\rho\text{ and }\delta(y)>100\rho,

where
C_{\epsilon}>0
is also independent of
\rho
,
x
and
y
.
The proof works
a little like when

x
is far from
y
, but we need to be a bit more careful about the Poincaré-Sobolev inequality
that we use.
Set again
r=2|x-y|
. Lemma
8.7
applied to the ball
B(x,r/20)
yields

(10.34)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq\frac{C}{m(B(x,r/2))}\int_{B(x,r/2)}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm\leq\frac{C}{m(B_{r})}\int_{B_{r}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm

and then for
s>0
and
R>r
to be chosen soon,

(10.35)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C\int_{0}^{s}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{r})}{m(B_{r})}dt+C\frac{m(B_{R})}{m(B_{r})}\int_{s}^{+\infty}\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(B_{R})}dt.

Take
R=\delta(y)
. The doubling property (
2.8
) allows us to estimate

\frac{m(B_{R})}{m(B_{r})}
by
\big(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\big)^{n}
.
Let
p
lie in the range given by Lemma
4.2
, and apply (
10.21
)
to estimate
m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})
; we get that

(10.36)

\frac{m(\Omega_{t}\cap B_{R})}{m(B_{R})}\leq Ct^{-p/2}R^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}\leq C_{p}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}\delta(y)^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}.

The bound (
10.35
) becomes now

(10.37)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)&\leq Cs+C_{p}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{n}\delta(y)^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}\int_{s}^{+\infty}t^{-\frac{p}{2}}dt\leq Cs+C_{p}\delta(y)^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)+n}r^{-n}s^{1-\frac{p}{2}}.\end{split}

We minimize then the right hand side of (
10.37
) in
s
. We take

s\approx\delta(y)^{1-d}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\frac{2n}{p}}
and get that

(10.38)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(x)\leq C_{p}\delta(y)^{1-d}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\frac{2n}{p}}.

The assertion (
10.32
) follows from (
10.38
) by taking
p=\frac{2n}{n-2}
(which is possible since
n\geq 3
) and by recalling that
w(y)=\delta(y)^{d+1-n}
. When
n=2
, we have
\delta(y)^{1-d}=\delta(y)^{n-d-1}=w(y)^{-1}
and so (
10.33
) is obtained from (
10.38
) by taking
p=\frac{2n}{\epsilon}<+\infty
.

Next we
give a bound on the
L^{q}
-norm of the gradient of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
for some
q>1
.
As before, we want the bound
to be independent of
\rho
so that we can later
let our Green function be a weak limit of a
subsequence of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
.

We want to prove first the following Caccioppoli-like inequality: for any
r>4\rho
,

(10.39)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}dm,

where
C>0
is a constant that depends only upon
d
,
n
,
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
.

Keep
r>4\rho
, and let
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that

\alpha\equiv 1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}
,
\alpha\equiv 0
on
B_{r/2}
and
|\nabla\alpha|\leq\frac{4}{r}
.
By construction,
g^{\rho}
lies in
W_{0}
, and thus the function
\varphi:=\alpha^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}

is supported in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{r/4}}
and lies in
W_{0}
thanks to Lemma
5.4
.
Since we like function with compact support, let us further multiply
\varphi
by a smooth, compactly
supported function
\psi_{R}
such that
\psi_{R}\equiv 1
on a large ball
B_{R}
.
Then
\psi_{R}\varphi
is compactly supported in
\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}
,
and still lies in
W_{0}
like
\varphi
.

Also, (
10.17
) says that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
lies in
W_{0}
and is a solution of
L\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}=0
in

\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}\supset\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{r/4}}
.
So we may apply the second item of Lemma
8.3
, with
E=\Omega\setminus\overline{B_{r/4}}
,
and we get that

(10.40)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla(\psi_{R}\varphi)\,dm=0,

but we would prefer to know that

(10.41)

\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dm=0.

Fortunately, we proved in (ii) of Lemma
5.5
that with correctly chosen functions
\psi_{R}
,
the product
\psi_{R}\varphi
tends to
\varphi
in
W
; see (
5.32
) in particular.
Then

(10.42)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot[\nabla\varphi-\nabla(\psi_{R}\varphi)]\,dm\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C||\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}||_{L^{2}(dm)}\,||\nabla\varphi-\nabla(\psi_{R}\varphi)||_{L^{2}(dm)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C||\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}||_{W}\,||\varphi-(\psi_{R}\varphi)||_{W}

by the boundedness property (
8.8
) of
\mathcal{A}
. The right-hand side tends to
0
,
so (
10.41
) follows from (
10.40
). Since
\varphi=\alpha^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
,
(
10.41
) yields

(10.43)

\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{2}[\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}]\,dm=-2\int_{\Omega}\alpha\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}[\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\alpha]\,dm.

Together with the elliptic and boundedness conditions on
\mathcal{A}
(see (
8.9
) and (
8.8
)) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
(
10.43
) becomes

(10.44)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{2}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm&\leq C\int_{\Omega}\alpha\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}||\nabla\alpha|\,dm\\
&\leq C\left(\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{2}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

which can be rewritten

(10.45)

\int_{\Omega}\alpha^{2}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}|\nabla\alpha|^{2}dm.

The bound (
10.39
) is then a straightforward consequence of our choice of
\alpha
.

Set
\hat{\Omega}_{t}=\{x\in\Omega,\,|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|>t\}
.
As before, there will be two different behaviors.
We first check that

(10.46)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Ct^{-\frac{d+1}{d}}\qquad\text{ when }t\leq\delta(y)^{-d}.

Let
r\geq\delta(y)

be given, to be chosen later.
The Caccioppoli-like inequality (
10.39
) and the pointwise bound (
10.28
) give

(10.47)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}dm\leq Cr^{-2d}m(B_{r})\leq Cr^{1-d}

by (
2.2
), and hence

(10.48)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t}\setminus B_{r})\leq Ct^{-2}r^{1-d}.

This yields

(10.49)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Ct^{-2}r^{1-d}+m(B_{r})=Ct^{-2}r^{1-d}+Cr^{1+d}

because
r\geq\delta(y)
. Take
r=t^{-\frac{1}{d}}

in (
10.49
) (and notice that
r\geq\delta(y)
when
t\leq\delta(y)^{-d}
).
The claim (
10.46
) follows.

We also want a version of (
10.46
) when
t
is big. We aim to prove that

(10.50)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Cw(y)^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}t^{-\frac{n}{n-1}}\qquad\text{ when $t\geq\delta(y)^{-d}$ and }\,n\geq 3

and for any
\eta\in(0,2)
,

(10.51)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq C_{\eta}w(y)^{-1}\delta(y)^{d\eta}t^{\eta-2}\qquad\text{when $t\geq\delta(y)^{-d}$ and }\,n=2.

The proof of (
10.50
) is similar to (
10.46
) but has an additional difficulty:
we cannot use the Caccioppoli-like argument (
10.39
) when
r
is smaller than
4\rho
.
So we will use another way. By
(
10.16
) for the test function
\phi=\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
and the elliptic condition (
8.9
),

(10.52)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq C\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm=C\fint_{B_{\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)\,dz\leq\frac{C}{m(B_{\rho})}\int_{B_{\rho}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\,dm

by
(
2.13
). Let
y_{0}
be such that
|y-y_{0}|=\delta(y)
. We use
Hölder’s inequality,
and then the Sobolev-Poincaré inequality (
4.13
),
with
p
in the range given by Lemma
4.2
, to get
that

(10.53)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm&\leq C_{p}m(B_{\rho})^{-1}m(B_{\rho})^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{B_{\rho}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq C_{p}m(B_{\rho})^{-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{B(y_{0},2\delta(y))}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{p}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&\leq C_{p}m(B_{\rho})^{-\frac{1}{p}}\delta(y)m(B_{3\delta(y)})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}\,dm\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

that is,

(10.54)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq C_{p}m(B_{\rho})^{-\frac{2}{p}}\delta(y)^{2}m(B_{\delta(y)})^{\frac{2}{p}-1}.

We use the fact that
100\rho<\delta(y)
and Lemma
2.3
to get that

m(B_{\rho})\approx\rho^{n}w(y)=\rho^{n}\delta(y)^{d+1-n}
. Besides, notice
that
m(B_{3\delta(y)})\approx\delta(y)^{d+1}
.
We end up with

(10.55)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq C_{p}\rho^{-\frac{2n}{p}}w(y)^{-\frac{2}{p}}\delta(y)^{2+(d+1)(\frac{2}{p}-1)}=C_{p}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{\rho}\right)^{\frac{2n}{p}}\delta(y)^{1-d}

once we recall that
w(y)=\delta(y)^{d+1-n}
. Observe
that the right-hand
side of (
10.55
) is similar to the one of (
10.38
). In the same way as below (
10.38
)
we take
p=\frac{2n}{n-2}

when
n\geq 3
and
p=\frac{4}{\epsilon}
when
n=2
, and obtain that

(10.56)

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Cw(y)^{-1}\rho^{2-n}&\text{ if }n\geq 3\\
C_{\epsilon}w(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{\rho}\right)^{\epsilon}&\text{ for any $\epsilon>0$ if $n=2$.}\end{array}\right.

Let
r\leq\delta(y)
, to be chosen soon. Now we show that

(10.57)

\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Cw(y)^{-1}r^{2-n}&\text{ if }n\geq 3\\
C_{\epsilon}w(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}&\text{ for any $\epsilon>0$ if $n=2$.}\end{array}\right.

When
r\leq 4\rho
, this
is a consequence of (
10.56
),
and when
4\rho<r\leq\delta(y)
, this
can be proven as we proved (
10.47
), by using Caccioppoli-like inequality (
10.39
) and the pointwise bounds (
10.32
) or (
10.33
).
That is, we say that

(10.58)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{r}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{2}dm&\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho})^{2}dm\\
&\leq r^{-2}m(B_{r})\frac{1}{w(y)^{2}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Cr^{2(2-n)}&\text{ if }n\geq 3\\
C_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{2\epsilon}&\text{ if }n=2,\,\epsilon>0\end{array}\right.\end{split}

and we observe that
m(B_{r})\approx w(y)r^{n}
.

Let
n\geq 3
. We deduce from (
10.57
) that

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t}\setminus B_{r})\leq Ct^{-2}r^{2-n}w(y)^{-1}
and then, since
m(B_{r})\leq Cr^{n}w(y)

and
thanks to Lemma
2.3
,

(10.59)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Cw(y)^{-1}t^{-2}r^{2-n}+m(B_{r})\leq Ct^{-2}w(y)^{-1}r^{2-n}+Cr^{n}w(y).

Choose
r=[tw(y)]^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}
(which is smaller than
\delta(y)
if
t\geq\delta(y)^{-d}
)
in (
10.59
). This
yields (
10.50
).

Let
n=2
and let
\eta\in(0,2)
be given. Set
\epsilon:=\frac{2\eta}{2-\eta}>0
. In this case, (
10.57
) gives

(10.60)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t}\setminus B_{r})\leq Ct^{-2}w(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}

and then since
m(B_{r})\leq Cr^{2}w(y)
by
Lemma
2.3
,

(10.61)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Ct^{-2}w(y)^{-1}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}+Cr^{2}w(y).

We want to minimize the above quantity in
r
. We take

r=\delta(y)^{\frac{2(1-d)+\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}}t^{-\frac{2}{2+\epsilon}}
, which is smaller than
\delta(y)
when
t\geq\delta(y)^{-d}
and we find that

(10.62)

m(\hat{\Omega}_{t})\leq Ct^{-\frac{4}{2+\epsilon}}\delta(y)^{\frac{2(1-d)+\epsilon(d+1)}{2+\epsilon}}=Ct^{\eta-2}\delta(y)^{1-d+\eta d},

with our choice of
\epsilon
.
Since
w(y)^{-1}=\delta(y)^{1-d}
when
n=2
, the claim (
10.51
) follows.

We plan to show now that
\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\in L^{q}(B_{R},w)
for
1\leq q<n/(n-1)
, and the
L^{q}(B_{R},w)
-norm of
\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}

can be bounded uniformly in
\rho
.
More precisely, we claim that for

R\geq\delta(y)
and

1\leq q<n/(n-1)
,

(10.63)

\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}dm\leq C_{q}R^{d(1-q)+1},

where
C_{q}
is independent of
\rho
and
R
.

Let
s\in(0,\delta(y)^{-d}]

be given, to be chosen soon. Then

(10.64)

\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}dm\leq C\int_{0}^{s}t^{q-1}m(B_{R})dt+C\int_{s}^{\delta(y)^{-d}}t^{q-1}m(\hat{\Omega}_{t}\cap B_{R})dt+C\int_{\delta(y)^{-d}}^{+\infty}t^{q-1}m(\hat{\Omega}_{t}\cap B_{R})dt.

Let us call
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
the three integrals
in the right hand side of (
10.64
).
By Lemma
2.3
,
I_{1}\leq Cs^{q}m(B_{R})\leq Cs^{q}R^{d+1}
.
The second integral
I_{2}
is bounded with the help of (
10.46
), which gives

(10.65)

I_{2}\leq C\int_{s}^{\delta(y)^{-d}}t^{q-1-\frac{d+1}{d}}dt\leq C\left(s^{q-\frac{d+1}{d}}-\delta(y)^{d(1-q)+1}\right).

When
n\geq 3
, the last integral
I_{3}
is bounded with the help of (
10.50
) and we obtain, when
q<\frac{n}{n-1}
,

(10.66)

I_{3}\leq Cw(y)^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}\int_{\delta(y)^{-d}}^{+\infty}t^{q-1-\frac{n}{n-1}}dt\leq Cw(y)^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}\delta(y)^{-qd+\frac{nd}{n-1}}=C\delta(y)^{1+d(1-q)}

where the last equality is obtained by using the fact that
w(y)=\delta(y)^{d+1-n}
. Note also that the same bound (
10.66
) can be obtained when
n=2
by using (
10.51
) with
\eta=\frac{2-q}{2}
. The left-hand side of (
10.64
) can be now bounded for every
n\geq 2
by

(10.67)

\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|^{q}dm\leq Cs^{q}R^{d+1}+C\left(s^{q-\frac{d+1}{d}}-\delta(y)^{d(1-q)+1}\right)+C\delta(y)^{1+d(1-q)}=Cs^{q}R^{d+1}+Cs^{q-\frac{d+1}{d}},

where the third term in the middle is dominated by
s^{q-\frac{d+1}{d}}
because
I_{2}\geq 0
.
We take
s=R^{-d}\leq\delta(y)^{-d}
in the right hand side of (
10.67
) to get the claim (
10.63
).

As we said, we want to define the Green function as a weak limit of functions
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}
,

0<\rho\leq\delta(y)/100
.
We want to prove that for

q\in(1,\frac{n}{n-1})
and

R>0
,

(10.68)

\|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\|_{W^{1,q}(B_{R})}\leq C_{q,R},

where
C_{q,R}
is independent of
\rho
(but depends, among others
things, on
y
,
q
and
R
).
First, it is enough to prove the result for
R\geq 2\delta(y)
. Thanks to (
10.63
), the quantity
\|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R},w)}
is bounded uniformly in
\rho\in(0,\delta(y)/100)
.
Due to (
2.13
), the quantity
\|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R})}
is bounded uniformly in
\rho
. Now, due to
[
Maz
, Corollary 1.1.11]
, we deduce that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\in W^{1,q}(B_{R})
and hence with the classical Poincaré inequality on balls that

(10.69)

\int_{B_{R}}\Big|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)-\fint_{B_{R}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)\,dy\Big|^{q}dz\leq C_{q,R}\|\nabla\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R})}^{q}\leq C_{q,R},

where
C_{q,R}>0
is
independent of
\rho
.
Choose
y_{0}\in\Gamma
such that
|y-y_{0}|=\delta(y_{0})
.
Note that
B(y_{0},\delta(y)/2)\subset B_{R}
because
R\geq 2\delta(y)
,
so (
10.69
) implies that

(10.70)

\Big|\fint_{B(y_{0},\delta(y)/2)}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)-\fint_{B_{R}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)\,dy\Big|^{q}\,dz\leq\int_{B_{R}}\Big|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)-\fint_{B_{R}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)\,dy\Big|^{q}\,dz\leq C_{q,R}

and hence also, by the triangle inequality,

(10.71)

\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)-\fint_{B(y_{0},\delta(y)/2)}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)\,dy\right|^{q}\,dz\leq C_{q,R}\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)-\fint_{B_{R}}\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)\,dy\right|^{q}\,dz.

Together with (
10.69
), we obtain

(10.72)

\int_{B_{R}}\left|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(z)\right|^{q}\,dz\leq C_{q,R}\left(1+\fint_{{B(y_{0},\delta(y)/2)}}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}(y)|\,dy\right)^{q}

and since (
10.28
) gives that
\fint_{{B(y_{0},\delta(y)/2)}}|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho}|\,dz\leq C\delta(y)^{1-d}
, the claim (
10.68
) follows.

Fix
q_{0}\in(1,\frac{n}{n-1})
, for instance, take
q_{0}=\frac{2n+1}{2n-1}
. Due to (
10.68
), for all
R>0
, the functions
(\mathfrak{g}_{\rho})_{0<100\rho<\delta(y)}
are uniformly bounded in
W^{1,q_{0}}(B_{R})
.
So a diagonal process allows us to find a sequence
(\rho_{\eta})_{\eta\geq 1}
converging to 0 and a function
\mathfrak{g}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

(10.73)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{g}=g(.,y)\ \text{in $W^{1,q_{0}}(B_{R})$, for all $R>0$.}

Let
q\in(1,\frac{n}{n-1})
and
R>0
. The functions
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
are uniformly bounded in
W^{1,q}(B_{R})
thanks to (
10.68
).
So we can find a subsequence
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
such that
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}}
converges weakly to some function
\mathfrak{g}^{(q,R)}\in W^{1,q}(B_{R})
.
Yet, by uniqueness of the limit,
\mathfrak{g}
equals
\mathfrak{g}^{(q,R)}
almost everywhere in
B_{R}
. As a consequence, up to a subsequence (that depends on
q
and
R
),

(10.74)

\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{g}=g(.,y)\ \text{in $W^{1,q}(B_{R})$}.

The assertion (
10.3
) follows.

We aim now to prove (
10.2
), that is

(10.75)

(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}\in W_{0}

whenever
\alpha\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
\alpha\equiv 1
on
B_{r}
for some
r>0
.

So we choose
\alpha\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
r>0
such that
\alpha\equiv 1
on
B_{r}
.
Since
\alpha
is compactly supported, we can find
R>0
such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\alpha\subset B_{R}
.
For any
\eta\in\mathbb{N}
such that

4\rho_{\eta}\leq r

and
100\rho_{\eta}<\delta(y)
,

(10.76)

\begin{split}\|(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{W}&\leq\|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\nabla\alpha\|_{L^{2}(B_{R}\setminus B_{r},w)}+\|(1-\alpha)\nabla g^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus B_{r},w)}\\
&\leq C_{\alpha}\sup_{B_{R}\setminus B_{r}}g^{\rho_{\eta}}+C_{\alpha}\|\nabla g^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus B_{r},w)}.\end{split}

Thanks to (
10.28
), (
10.32
) and (
10.33
), the term

\sup_{B_{R}\setminus B_{r}}g^{\rho_{\eta}}
can be bounded by a constant that doesn’t depend on
\eta
,
provided that
\rho_{\eta}\leq\min(r/4,\delta(y)/100)
.
In the same way, (
10.57
) proves that
\|\nabla g^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus B_{r},w)}

can be also bounded by a constant independent of
\eta
.
As a consequence, for any
\eta
satisfying
4\rho_{\eta}\leq r
,

(10.77)

\|(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{W}\leq C_{\alpha}

where
C_{\alpha}
is independent of
\eta
.
Note also that for
\eta
large enough,
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
belongs to
W_{0}

because
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\in W_{0}
by construction, and by Lemma
5.4
.
Therefore, the functions

(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
,
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
large, lie in a fixed closed ball of the Hilbert space
W_{0}
.
So, up to a subsequence, there exists
f_{\alpha}\in W_{0}
such that
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\rightharpoonup f_{\alpha}
in
W_{0}
. By uniqueness of the limit, we have
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}=f_{\alpha}\in W_{0}
, that is

(10.78)

(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\rightharpoonup(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}\quad\text{ in }W_{0}.

The claim (
10.75
) follows.

Observe
that (
10.75
) implies that
\mathfrak{g}\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\})
. Indeed, take
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\})
. We can find
r>0
such that
\varphi\equiv 0
in
B_{r}
. Construct now
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{r})
such that
\alpha\equiv 1
in
B_{r/2}
and we have

(10.79)

\varphi\mathfrak{g}=\varphi[(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}]\in W_{0}\subset W

by
(
10.75
) and Lemma
5.4
. Hence
\mathfrak{g}\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\})
.

Now we want to prove (
10.4
). Fix

q\in(1,n/(n-1))
and a function
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{\delta(y)/2})
such that

\phi\equiv 1
in
B_{\delta(y)/4}
. Then let

\varphi
be any function in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
. Let us first check that

(10.80)

a(\mathfrak{g},\phi\varphi):=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla\mathfrak{g}\cdot\nabla[\phi\varphi]dx=\varphi(y)

and

(10.81)

a(\mathfrak{g},(1-\phi)\varphi):=\int_{\Omega}A\nabla\mathfrak{g}\cdot\nabla[(1-\phi)\varphi]dx=0.

The map
a(.,\phi\varphi)
is a bounded linear functional on
W^{1,q}(B_{\delta(y)/2})
and thus the weak convergence (in
W^{1,q}(B_{R})
) of a subsequence
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
yields

(10.82)

a(\mathfrak{g},\phi\varphi)=\lim_{\eta^{\prime}\to+\infty}a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}},\phi\varphi)=\lim_{\rho\to 0}\fint_{B(y,\rho)}\phi\varphi\,dx=\varphi(y),

which is (
10.80
). Let
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{\delta(y)/4})

be such that

\alpha\equiv 1
on
B_{\delta(y)/8}
. The map
a(.,(1-\phi)\varphi)
is bounded on
W_{0}
thus the weak convergence of a subsequence of
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
to
(1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}
in
W_{0}
gives

(10.83)

\begin{split}a(\mathfrak{g},(1-\phi)\varphi)&=a((1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g},(1-\phi)\varphi)\\
&=\lim_{\eta^{\prime}\to+\infty}a((1-\alpha)\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}},(1-\phi)\varphi)=\lim_{\eta^{\prime}\to+\infty}a(\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta^{\prime}}},(1-\phi)\varphi)\\
&=\lim_{\rho\to 0}\fint_{B(y,\rho)}(1-\phi)\varphi\,dx=0.\end{split}

which is (
10.81
). The assertion (
10.4
)
now follows from (
10.80
) and (
10.81
).

If we use (
10.4
) for the functions in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega\setminus\{y\})
,
we immediately obtain that

(10.84)

\mathfrak{g}\text{ is a solution of $L\mathfrak{g}=0$ on $\Omega\setminus\{y\}$.}

Assertions (
10.5
) and (
10.7
) come from the weak lower semicontinuity of the

L^{q}
-norms and the bounds (
10.47
), (
10.57
) and (
10.63
).
Notice also that
r^{1-d}\approx\frac{r^{2-n}}{w(y)}
when
r
is near
\delta(y)
, so the cut-off
between the different cases does not need to be so precise.
Let us show (
10.6
). Let
R>0
be a big given number.
We have shown that the sequence
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
is uniformly bounded in
W^{1,q}(B_{R})
. Then,
by the
Rellich-Kondrachov theorem, there exists a subsequence of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
that also
converges strongly in
L^{1}(B_{R})
and then another subsequence of
\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}
that converges almost everywhere in
B_{R}
.
The estimates (
10.28
), (
10.32
) and (
10.33
) yield then

(10.85)

0\leq\mathfrak{g}(x)\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C|x-y|^{1-d}&\text{ if }4|x-y|\geq\delta(y)\\
\frac{C|x-y|^{2-n}}{w(y)}&\text{ if }2|x-y|\leq\delta(y),\,n\geq 3\\
\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{w(y)}\left(\frac{\delta(y)}{|x-y|}\right)^{\epsilon}&\text{ if }2|x-y|\leq\delta(y),\,n=2,\end{array}\right.\quad\text{ a.e. on }B_{R}.

But by (
10.84
)
\mathfrak{g}
is a solution of
L\mathfrak{g}=0
on
\Omega\setminus\{y\}
, so it
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\}
by Lemmas
8.8
and
8.16
, and the bounds
(
10.85
) actually hold pointwise in
\Omega\cap B_{R}\setminus\{y\}
.
Since
R
can be chosen as large as we want, the bounds (
10.6
) follow.

It remains to check the weak estimates (
10.8
) and (
10.9
).
Set
q=\frac{2n+1}{2n-1}
, which satisfies
1<q<\frac{n}{n-1}<\frac{n}{n-2}
.
Let
t>0
be given ; by the weak
lower semicontinuity of the
L^{q}
-norm,

(10.86)

t^{q}\,\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t)}{m(B_{R})}\leq\frac{1}{m(B_{R})}\|\mathfrak{g}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R},w)}^{q}\leq\liminf_{\eta\to+\infty}\frac{1}{m(B_{R})}\|\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}\|_{L^{q}(B_{R},w)}^{q}.

Let us use
[
Duo
, p. 28, Proposition 2.3]
; in the case of (
10.8
), we could manage otherwise,
but we also want to get (
10.9
) with the same proof. We observe that

(10.87)

\displaystyle t^{q}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t\})}{m(B_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{\eta\to+\infty}\left[\int_{0}^{t}s^{q-1}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t,\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}>s)\}}{m(B_{R})}ds\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\int_{t}^{+\infty}s^{q-1}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t,\,\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}>s\})}{m(B_{R})}ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t^{q}}{q}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t\})}{m(B_{R})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\liminf_{\eta\to+\infty}\int_{t}^{+\infty}s^{q-1}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}>s\})}{m(B_{R})}ds.

Let
p
lie in the range given by Lemma
4.2
. The bounds (
10.21
) gives

(10.88)

\begin{split}t^{q}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\,\mathfrak{g}(x)>t\})}{m(B_{R})}&\leq C\liminf_{\eta\to+\infty}\int_{t}^{+\infty}s^{q-1}\frac{m(\{x\in B_{R},\mathfrak{g}^{\rho_{\eta}}>s\})}{m(B_{R})}ds\\
&\leq C_{p}R^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}\int_{t}^{+\infty}s^{q-1-\frac{p}{2}}ds\leq C_{p}R^{\frac{p}{2}(1-d)}t^{q-\frac{p}{2}}.\end{split}

The estimates (
10.8
) follows by dividing both sides of (
10.88
) by
t^{q}
. The same ideas are used to prove (
10.9
) from (
10.46
), (
10.50
) and (
10.51
).
This finally completes the proof of Lemma
10.1
.

\Box

Lemma 10.5
.

Any non-negative function
g:\,\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}

that verifies
the following conditions:

(i)

for every

y\in\Omega
and

\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\alpha\equiv 1
in
B(y,r)
for some
r>0
, the function
(1-\alpha)g(.,y)
lies
in
W_{0}
,

(ii)

for every

y\in\Omega
, the function
g(.,y)
lies
in
W^{1,1}(B(y,\delta(y)))
,

(iii)

for

y\in\Omega
and

\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,

(10.89)

\int_{\Omega}A\nabla_{x}g(x,y)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)dx=\varphi(y),

enjoys the following pointwise lower bound:

(10.90)

g(x,y)\geq C^{-1}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{m(B(y,|x-y|))}\approx\frac{|x-y|^{2-n}}{w(y)}\ \text{ for $x,y\in\Omega$ such that }0<|x-y|\leq\frac{\delta(y)}{2}.

Proof
.  Let
g
satisfy the assumptions of the lemma, fix
y\in\Omega
, write
\mathfrak{g}(x)
for
g(x,y)
, and
use
B_{r}
for
B(y,r)
.
Thus we want to prove that

(10.91)

\mathfrak{g}(x)\geq\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{Cm(B_{|x-y|})}\qquad\text{ whenever }0<|x-y|\leq\frac{\delta(y)}{2}.

With our assumptions,
\mathfrak{g}\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{y\})
and it is
a solution in
\Omega\setminus\{y\}
with zero trace;
the proof is the same as for (
10.79
) and (
10.84
) in Lemma
10.1
.
Take
x\in\Omega\setminus\{y\}
such that
|x-y|\leq\frac{\delta(y)}{2}
.
Write
r
for
|x-y|
and let
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega\setminus\{y\})
be
such that
\alpha=1
on
B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}
,
\alpha=0

outside of
B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}
,
and
|\nabla\alpha|\leq 8/r
. Using Caccioppoli’s
inequality (Lemma
8.6
) with the cut-off function
\alpha
, we obtain

(10.92)

\begin{split}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}|^{2}dm&\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}}\mathfrak{g}^{2}dm\\
&\leq Cr^{-2}m(B_{3r/2})\sup_{B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}}\mathfrak{g}^{2}\leq Cr^{-2}m(B_{r})\sup_{B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}}\mathfrak{g}^{2}\end{split}

by
the doubling property (
2.8
). We can cover
B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}
by a finite
(independent of
y
and
r
) number of balls of radius
r/20
centered in
B_{3r/2}\setminus B_{r/4}
.
Then use the Harnack inequality given by Lemma
8.9
several times, to get that

(10.93)

\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}|^{2}dm\leq Cr^{-2}m(B_{r})\mathfrak{g}(x)^{2}.

Define
another function
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
which
is supported in
B_{r}
, equal to
1
on
B_{r/2}
, and such that

|\nabla\eta|\leq\frac{4}{r}
.
Use
\eta
as a test function in (
10.89
) to get that

(10.94)

1=\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}\mathcal{A}\nabla\mathfrak{g}\cdot\nabla\eta\,dm\leq\frac{C}{r}\int_{B_{r}\setminus B_{r/2}}|\nabla\mathfrak{g}|\,dm,

where we used (
8.8
) for the last estimate.
Together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
10.93
), this yields

(10.95)
